I'm still managing my job as an engineering manager, and I'm keeping up with my basic responsibilities around the house, so it hasn't completely derailed my life. But... it's definitely affecting my social life and how I see myself. \n\nI've been turning down plans lately. I used to go to the theater with friends or join my walking club, but I just... I feel too ashamed right now. I'm so preoccupied with how I look, worrying if people can tell what I'm doing, or if I look disciplined enough. It just feels easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes or your response to them caused any problems with your job performance, family responsibilities, or other important areas of your life besides your social life and how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I mentioned, work is... work is fine. I'm still managing my team without any issues, getting all my projects done. And as for family or things around the house, I'm still keeping up with all that, too. It hasn't gotten in the way there. \n\nIt's really just... just the social stuff I talked about, and being stuck in my own head about it. So, no, it hasn't caused problems in those other areas.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating happen specifically at night, and do you feel like you're waking up to eat or have a strong urge to eat only after dinner or in the middle of the night?\n\nPatient: No, it's not like that. I don't wake up in the middle of the night to eat, and it's not just an after-dinner thing. The eating can really happen at any time during the day... it just depends on when the urge hits me, I guess. It's not tied to nighttime or waking up from sleep.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what it feels like when you start eating and can’t stop? Do you feel like you’re completely unable to control how much you eat during those times, like it’s happening automatically and you can’t stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly what it is. It's a really strange feeling. Once I start eating... it feels like I can almost see myself doing it. \n\nI'll be sitting there, and in my head, I'm clearly telling myself to stop. I know I should stop. But it's like I have no control over my own body at that point. My hands just keep going back for more food, no matter what I'm thinking. It definitely feels automatic, like I just can't stop myself once it starts.\n\nDoctor: When you're not having these eating episodes, do you find yourself constantly thinking about food, planning when or what you'll eat next, or feeling anxious if you're not eating as you expect to?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, when I'm not in the middle of one of those episodes, I'm just going about my day. I'm focused on work or whatever else I have going on. I don't sit around obsessing over my next meal or planning out what I'm going to eat. And I don't really get anxious about food if my schedule changes or anything. It's really just those specific times when the urge hits and I lose control. Otherwise, I don't think about food all that much.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you’re not in control of your eating, like you can’t stop eating even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago... yes. That's exactly how it is. I really have no control over it once it starts. Even if I want to stop, and I'm telling myself in my head to stop, I just can't.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I'll go out and end up walking miles past my usual route to try and work it off... I just keep going, even when my knees are aching and telling me I should really just head home.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes upset you a lot or interfere with your daily life, like affecting your work, relationships, or how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely upsets me. I'm still managing my job as an engineering manager, and I'm keeping up with my basic responsibilities around the house, so it hasn't completely derailed my life. But... it's definitely affecting my social life and how I see myself. \n\nI've been turning down plans lately. I used to go to the theater with friends or join my walking club, but I just... I feel too ashamed right now. I'm so preoccupied with how I look, worrying if people can tell what I'm doing, or if I look disciplined enough. It just feels easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes or your response to them caused any problems with your job performance, family responsibilities, or other important areas of your life besides your social life and how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I mentioned, work is... work is fine. I'm still managing my team without any issues, getting all my projects done. And as for family or things around the house, I'm still keeping up with all that, too. It hasn't gotten in the way there. \n\nIt's really just... just the social stuff I talked about, and being stuck in my own head about it. So, no, it hasn't caused problems in those other areas.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating happen specifically at night, and do you feel like you're waking up to eat or have a strong urge to eat only after dinner or in the middle of the night?\n\nPatient: No, it's not like that. I don't wake up in the middle of the night to eat, and it's not just an after-dinner thing. The eating can really happen at any time during the day... it just depends on when the urge hits me, I guess. It's not tied to nighttime or waking up from sleep.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what it feels like when you start eating and can’t stop? Do you feel like you’re completely unable to control how much you eat during those times, like it’s happening automatically and you can’t stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly what it is. It's a really strange feeling. Once I start eating... it feels like I can almost see myself doing it. \n\nI'll be sitting there, and in my head, I'm clearly telling myself to stop. I know I should stop. But it's like I have no control over my own body at that point. My hands just keep going back for more food, no matter what I'm thinking. It definitely feels automatic, like I just can't stop myself once it starts.\n\nDoctor: When you're not having these eating episodes, do you find yourself constantly thinking about food, planning when or what you'll eat next, or feeling anxious if you're not eating as you expect to?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, when I'm not in the middle of one of those episodes, I'm just going about my day. I'm focused on work or whatever else I have going on. I don't sit around obsessing over my next meal or planning out what I'm going to eat. And I don't really get anxious about food if my schedule changes or anything. It's really just those specific times when the urge hits and I lose control. Otherwise, I don't think about food all that much.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you’re not in control of your eating, like you can’t stop eating even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago... yes. That's exactly how it is. I really have no control over it once it starts. Even if I want to stop, and I'm telling myself in my head to stop, I just can't.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these eating episodes, do you ever feel a similar sense of losing control, like your body is doing something on its own without your control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, when I'm at work or just doing things around the house, I feel completely in control. It's really just... just tied to the eating. Outside of those specific times, my body does exactly what I tell it to do. I don't have that weird automatic feeling with anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes cause you significant distress specifically in social situations, such as fear of others noticing or embarrassment about your eating behavior when you're around people?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say that happens. I mean, I don't really have these episodes when I'm around other people. If I'm out at a work dinner or eating with family, I just eat a normal amount. So I don't really have that fear of people noticing or getting embarrassed in social situations, because... well, it really only happens when I'm by myself.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're in a daze or not completely aware of what you're doing while you're eating?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm... I'm completely aware of what I'm doing. It's not like I black out or go into a trance or anything like that. I know exactly what I'm eating, and I know that I'm doing it. Like I said, I just can't seem to make myself stop. But my mind is clear and I'm definitely aware the whole time it's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes usually happen in response to feeling stressed, anxious, sad, or experiencing other strong emotions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm sure I've had them when I was stressed from work or something, but it's not like a direct trigger. It just... happens. I could be having a perfectly fine day, no stress, no bad mood, and I'll still end up eating like that. So, no, I wouldn't tie it to any specific emotion.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's strange, it feels like I can almost see myself doing it, and in my head, I'm telling myself to stop, but my hands just keep going back for more food. I just can't seem to stop once I get started.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating, do you ever try to compensate for the food you ate, like making yourself vomit, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don't make myself throw up or use laxatives, no. But I do try to make up for it. \n\nUsually, after a bad eating night, I’ll tell myself I’m fixing it by just skipping breakfast and lunch the next day. And I definitely push the exercise. I'll go out and end up walking miles past my usual route to try and work it off... I just keep going, even when my knees are aching and telling me I should really just head home.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes upset you a lot or interfere with your daily life, like affecting your work, relationships, or how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely upsets me. I'm still managing my job as an engineering manager, and I'm keeping up with my basic responsibilities around the house, so it hasn't completely derailed my life. But... it's definitely affecting my social life and how I see myself. \n\nI've been turning down plans lately. I used to go to the theater with friends or join my walking club, but I just... I feel too ashamed right now. I'm so preoccupied with how I look, worrying if people can tell what I'm doing, or if I look disciplined enough. It just feels easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes or your response to them caused any problems with your job performance, family responsibilities, or other important areas of your life besides your social life and how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I mentioned, work is... work is fine. I'm still managing my team without any issues, getting all my projects done. And as for family or things around the house, I'm still keeping up with all that, too. It hasn't gotten in the way there. \n\nIt's really just... just the social stuff I talked about, and being stuck in my own head about it. So, no, it hasn't caused problems in those other areas.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating happen specifically at night, and do you feel like you're waking up to eat or have a strong urge to eat only after dinner or in the middle of the night?\n\nPatient: No, it's not like that. I don't wake up in the middle of the night to eat, and it's not just an after-dinner thing. The eating can really happen at any time during the day... it just depends on when the urge hits me, I guess. It's not tied to nighttime or waking up from sleep.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what it feels like when you start eating and can’t stop? Do you feel like you’re completely unable to control how much you eat during those times, like it’s happening automatically and you can’t stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly what it is. It's a really strange feeling. Once I start eating... it feels like I can almost see myself doing it. \n\nI'll be sitting there, and in my head, I'm clearly telling myself to stop. I know I should stop. But it's like I have no control over my own body at that point. My hands just keep going back for more food, no matter what I'm thinking. It definitely feels automatic, like I just can't stop myself once it starts.\n\nDoctor: When you're not having these eating episodes, do you find yourself constantly thinking about food, planning when or what you'll eat next, or feeling anxious if you're not eating as you expect to?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, when I'm not in the middle of one of those episodes, I'm just going about my day. I'm focused on work or whatever else I have going on. I don't sit around obsessing over my next meal or planning out what I'm going to eat. And I don't really get anxious about food if my schedule changes or anything. It's really just those specific times when the urge hits and I lose control. Otherwise, I don't think about food all that much.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you’re not in control of your eating, like you can’t stop eating even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago... yes. That's exactly how it is. I really have no control over it once it starts. Even if I want to stop, and I'm telling myself in my head to stop, I just can't.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these eating episodes, do you ever feel a similar sense of losing control, like your body is doing something on its own without your control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, when I'm at work or just doing things around the house, I feel completely in control. It's really just... just tied to the eating. Outside of those specific times, my body does exactly what I tell it to do. I don't have that weird automatic feeling with anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes cause you significant distress specifically in social situations, such as fear of others noticing or embarrassment about your eating behavior when you're around people?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say that happens. I mean, I don't really have these episodes when I'm around other people. If I'm out at a work dinner or eating with family, I just eat a normal amount. So I don't really have that fear of people noticing or getting embarrassed in social situations, because... well, it really only happens when I'm by myself.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're in a daze or not completely aware of what you're doing while you're eating?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm... I'm completely aware of what I'm doing. It's not like I black out or go into a trance or anything like that. I know exactly what I'm eating, and I know that I'm doing it. Like I said, I just can't seem to make myself stop. But my mind is clear and I'm definitely aware the whole time it's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes usually happen in response to feeling stressed, anxious, sad, or experiencing other strong emotions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm sure I've had them when I was stressed from work or something, but it's not like a direct trigger. It just... happens. I could be having a perfectly fine day, no stress, no bad mood, and I'll still end up eating like that. So, no, I wouldn't tie it to any specific emotion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense distress specifically about not being able to control your eating during these episodes, like it makes you feel helpless or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel intense distress or helpless about it. I mean, I know I can't stop once I start, and that's frustrating, but it doesn't overwhelm me. It's just something that happens. I don't sit there feeling panicked or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, would you say that you feel a complete loss of control over your eating, like you can't stop even though you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. It's actually what brings me in here to see you. Once I start eating at night... it feels like I can see myself doing it, and I'm in my head telling myself to stop, but my hands just keep going back for more. I want to stop, but I just can't make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel detached from yourself or like you're watching yourself eat without being able to intervene, almost like you're outside of your own body?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I did say it feels like I can see myself doing it, but I didn't mean it literally like I'm floating outside my body or anything weird like that. I just meant I'm fully aware of what I'm doing. I'm right there in my body, eating, and knowing I shouldn't be, but I just don't stop. So, no, I don't feel detached or outside of myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just keep going, even when my knees are aching and telling me I should really just head home.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes upset you a lot or interfere with your daily life, like affecting your work, relationships, or how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely upsets me. I'm still managing my job as an engineering manager, and I'm keeping up with my basic responsibilities around the house, so it hasn't completely derailed my life. But... it's definitely affecting my social life and how I see myself. \n\nI've been turning down plans lately. I used to go to the theater with friends or join my walking club, but I just... I feel too ashamed right now. I'm so preoccupied with how I look, worrying if people can tell what I'm doing, or if I look disciplined enough. It just feels easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes or your response to them caused any problems with your job performance, family responsibilities, or other important areas of your life besides your social life and how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I mentioned, work is... work is fine. I'm still managing my team without any issues, getting all my projects done. And as for family or things around the house, I'm still keeping up with all that, too. It hasn't gotten in the way there. \n\nIt's really just... just the social stuff I talked about, and being stuck in my own head about it. So, no, it hasn't caused problems in those other areas.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating happen specifically at night, and do you feel like you're waking up to eat or have a strong urge to eat only after dinner or in the middle of the night?\n\nPatient: No, it's not like that. I don't wake up in the middle of the night to eat, and it's not just an after-dinner thing. The eating can really happen at any time during the day... it just depends on when the urge hits me, I guess. It's not tied to nighttime or waking up from sleep.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what it feels like when you start eating and can’t stop? Do you feel like you’re completely unable to control how much you eat during those times, like it’s happening automatically and you can’t stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly what it is. It's a really strange feeling. Once I start eating... it feels like I can almost see myself doing it. \n\nI'll be sitting there, and in my head, I'm clearly telling myself to stop. I know I should stop. But it's like I have no control over my own body at that point. My hands just keep going back for more food, no matter what I'm thinking. It definitely feels automatic, like I just can't stop myself once it starts.\n\nDoctor: When you're not having these eating episodes, do you find yourself constantly thinking about food, planning when or what you'll eat next, or feeling anxious if you're not eating as you expect to?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, when I'm not in the middle of one of those episodes, I'm just going about my day. I'm focused on work or whatever else I have going on. I don't sit around obsessing over my next meal or planning out what I'm going to eat. And I don't really get anxious about food if my schedule changes or anything. It's really just those specific times when the urge hits and I lose control. Otherwise, I don't think about food all that much.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you’re not in control of your eating, like you can’t stop eating even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago... yes. That's exactly how it is. I really have no control over it once it starts. Even if I want to stop, and I'm telling myself in my head to stop, I just can't.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these eating episodes, do you ever feel a similar sense of losing control, like your body is doing something on its own without your control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, when I'm at work or just doing things around the house, I feel completely in control. It's really just... just tied to the eating. Outside of those specific times, my body does exactly what I tell it to do. I don't have that weird automatic feeling with anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes cause you significant distress specifically in social situations, such as fear of others noticing or embarrassment about your eating behavior when you're around people?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say that happens. I mean, I don't really have these episodes when I'm around other people. If I'm out at a work dinner or eating with family, I just eat a normal amount. So I don't really have that fear of people noticing or getting embarrassed in social situations, because... well, it really only happens when I'm by myself.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're in a daze or not completely aware of what you're doing while you're eating?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm... I'm completely aware of what I'm doing. It's not like I black out or go into a trance or anything like that. I know exactly what I'm eating, and I know that I'm doing it. Like I said, I just can't seem to make myself stop. But my mind is clear and I'm definitely aware the whole time it's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes usually happen in response to feeling stressed, anxious, sad, or experiencing other strong emotions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm sure I've had them when I was stressed from work or something, but it's not like a direct trigger. It just... happens. I could be having a perfectly fine day, no stress, no bad mood, and I'll still end up eating like that. So, no, I wouldn't tie it to any specific emotion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense distress specifically about not being able to control your eating during these episodes, like it makes you feel helpless or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel intense distress or helpless about it. I mean, I know I can't stop once I start, and that's frustrating, but it doesn't overwhelm me. It's just something that happens. I don't sit there feeling panicked or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, would you say that you feel a complete loss of control over your eating, like you can't stop even though you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. It's actually what brings me in here to see you. Once I start eating at night... it feels like I can see myself doing it, and I'm in my head telling myself to stop, but my hands just keep going back for more. I want to stop, but I just can't make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel detached from yourself or like you're watching yourself eat without being able to intervene, almost like you're outside of your own body?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I did say it feels like I can see myself doing it, but I didn't mean it literally like I'm floating outside my body or anything weird like that. I just meant I'm fully aware of what I'm doing. I'm right there in my body, eating, and knowing I shouldn't be, but I just don't stop. So, no, I don't feel detached or outside of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes usually happen when you're by yourself, and do you tend to eat much more in secret?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they pretty much always happen when I'm alone. I don't really eat like that around other people. If my wife is home or if we have company, I just eat a normal amount. But if she goes to bed early or goes out of town, that's when it happens. I definitely keep it to myself. I wouldn't want anyone to see me eating that much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
